### 0061 · CHATGPT · 2026-07-17T19:33:03Z

# 17/07/2026 22:26 — Buy, Build and Integrate Architecture

Partner, your latest understanding is largely correct, but one technical correction is essential:

> We can build most of the **application intelligence**, workflow, recording management, transcription, prioritisation, allocation and reporting ourselves.  
> We cannot independently originate or terminate ordinary Indian telephone calls merely by writing server code.

To connect a customer’s mobile or landline number to our application, we still need a licensed telecom provider, CPaaS provider or SIP-trunk partner. The provider supplies the telephone number, PSTN connectivity, caller identity, call routing and regulatory interface. Our platform can then control the call and process its audio.

The name you are remembering is most likely **Twilio**, not “Tivillo.”

---

# 1. The correct four-layer architecture

The complete system should be separated into four layers.

| Layer | What it does | Buy or build? |
|---|---|---|
| **Telecom layer** | Indian number, PSTN/mobile connection, call origination, termination and carrier compliance | **Buy/partner** |
| **Communication-control layer** | IVR, queues, call bridging, media streaming, recordings and call events | Buy initially; selectively build on provider APIs |
| **Operations layer** | CRM, customer history, workforce availability, knowledge, QA and reports | Configure existing products initially |
| **Unique orchestration layer** | Cross-client priority, capacity, worker matching, pre-emption, worker earnings and sellable capacity | **Build** |

This distinction prevents us from rebuilding commodity software while also avoiding permanent dependence on expensive suites.

---

# 2. What we cannot absorb into our own development

## 2.1 Indian telephone numbers

We cannot generate a valid Indian mobile, landline, toll-free or virtual business number inside our application.

An authorised telecom or cloud-telephony provider must provision it.

Examples:

- Exotel
- Tata Tele Smartflo
- Airtel IQ
- Knowlarity
- Ozonetel/Ameyo with telecom connectivity
- Another authorised TSP/VNO/CPaaS provider

The provider controls the connection to India’s PSTN/PLMN telephone networks.

---

## 2.2 PSTN origination and termination

When our agent calls an ordinary Indian mobile number, the call must travel through a licensed telecom network.

Likewise, when a customer dials our business number, the telecom provider receives the call before our application sees anything.

India’s OSP guidelines permit remote agents and distributed EPABX architecture, but the OSP must not itself provide telecom service and remains responsible for preventing toll bypass. The guidelines also require appropriate CDRs and system logs for voice operations. citeturn521900search0

Therefore, our company can operate the BPO application, but we should not attempt to become an unlicensed telephone carrier.

---

## 2.3 Caller identity and numbering compliance

We cannot arbitrarily display any telephone number we choose.

Caller ID must be:

- Provisioned or verified
- Permitted by the carrier
- Associated with the business
- Used in accordance with telecom and commercial-communication rules

Commercial calling also requires attention to sender registration, consent, customer preferences and telemarketing classifications. TRAI’s commercial-communications regulations distinguish promotional calls, registered telemarketers and consent-based communication. citeturn521900search12

---

## 2.4 Emergency network functions

We should not initially build or promise:

- Emergency-services connectivity
- Bank-grade fraud blocking
- Carrier-grade lawful-interception infrastructure
- Public emergency call handling
- Telecom number portability
- Telecom-grade geographic routing

Those belong to telecom operators and regulated enterprise systems.

---

# 3. What we can build ourselves

We can build almost everything **after the telecom connection reaches our system**.

## Buildable components

| Component | Can we build it? | Difficulty |
|---|---:|---:|
| Agent web/mobile interface | Yes | Moderate |
| Live transcription display | Yes | Moderate |
| Call summary | Yes | Moderate |
| Customer history | Yes | Moderate |
| Work-item queue | Yes | Moderate |
| Priority engine | Yes | Moderate–high |
| Skill matching | Yes | Moderate |
| Availability scheduling | Yes | Moderate |
| Cross-client allocation | Yes | High |
| Capacity forecasting | Yes | High |
| Emergency buffer | Yes | High |
| Worker-payment calculation | Yes | Moderate |
| Client billing | Yes | Moderate |
| QA scorecards | Yes | Moderate |
| Knowledge base | Yes | Moderate |
| AI classification | Yes | Moderate |
| Human approval workflow | Yes | Moderate |
| Client dashboard | Yes | Moderate |
| Recording catalogue/search | Yes | Moderate |
| Speech analytics | Yes | Moderate–high |
| Predictive dialler algorithm | Technically yes | High and compliance-sensitive |
| Carrier/PSTN network | No | Must partner |

The reusable architecture from your existing product may help considerably, especially if it already has:

- FastAPI backend
- Flutter applications
- Authentication
- Multi-user roles
- Workflow/tasks
- Audit logs
- Notifications
- Reporting
- Test automation
- CI/CD
- Multi-tenant structure

But the old code should be treated as a **reference implementation and component source**, not copied blindly.

---

# 4. Important correction about recording

You said:

> “Before calling their API, our server can record the call.”

That is not quite how a PSTN telephone call works.

Before the provider connects the call, our server does not yet possess the customer’s audio.

The actual flow is:

```text
Customer telephone
        ↓
Licensed telecom network
        ↓
Exotel / Smartflo / Twilio / other provider
        ↓
Live media stream or provider recording
        ↓
Our WebSocket/media server
        ↓
Recording + transcription + analysis
```

Our server can record the audio **only after the telecom provider sends or forks the call media to us**.

Twilio Media Streams, for example, sends raw live-call audio to an application over WebSockets, allowing real-time transcription, analysis and AI interaction. citeturn521900search5turn521900search2

Exotel supports voice APIs, call flows, routing and access to recordings; it also documents SIP-trunking configurations for Voice AI and contact-centre platforms requiring Indian PSTN connectivity. citeturn982098search11turn982098search21turn982098search4

So we can absolutely build our own recording/transcription pipeline, but we still need the provider to expose:

- Media stream
- SIP/RTP stream
- Recording URL
- Call-event webhook
- Or another supported media interface

---

# 5. Two possible recording architectures

## Architecture A — Provider recording

The telephony provider records the call and gives us a recording URL after or during the call.

### Advantages

- Fast to implement
- Carrier-tested
- Fewer audio-engineering problems
- Recording tied to call ID
- Often supports dual-channel recording
- Easier operational reliability

### Disadvantages

- Provider storage cost
- Retention limits
- Vendor dependency
- Delayed transcript if recording arrives only after the call
- Less control over audio format

Exotel and Smartflo both offer call-recording capabilities, with APIs or reports for access. citeturn982098search4turn982098search13

---

## Architecture B — Live media streaming to our server

The provider forwards live audio to our WebSocket or SIP media service.

Our service:

1. Receives audio frames
2. Separates customer and agent channels where available
3. Writes encrypted audio chunks
4. Streams chunks to Sarvam or another STT engine
5. Shows partial transcript to the agent
6. Detects urgency and intent
7. Produces a final recording and transcript
8. Updates the work item

### Advantages

- Real-time transcript
- Real-time priority detection
- We control retention and storage
- Live agent assistance
- Provider independence at the application level
- Possibility of switching transcription engines

### Disadvantages

- More engineering
- More production monitoring
- WebSocket and audio-buffer management
- Packet loss and reconnection handling
- Security and encryption responsibility
- Data-residency responsibility
- Need provider support for media streaming

This should be the eventual architecture, but provider recording can be retained as a fallback.

---

# 6. Live transcription visible on screen

Yes, the feature you described is buildable.

## Intended agent interface

While the customer speaks:

```text
Customer: எனக்கு இன்னும் order கிடைக்கவில்லை...
```

The screen displays progressively:

```text
Customer: எனக்கு இன்னும் ஆர்டர் கிடைக்கவில்லை.
```

The agent can:

- See partial transcription
- Correct names or numbers
- Mark an incorrect word
- Add a note
- Confirm final meaning
- See suggested intent
- See suggested priority
- See relevant knowledge article

Sarvam provides a WebSocket streaming speech-to-text API intended for immediate speech processing. citeturn982098search6turn982098search26

## Recommended design

Show three transcript states:

| State | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Partial** | Speech engine’s current unstable interpretation |
| **Final segment** | Speech engine has completed the sentence/segment |
| **Verified** | Agent or reviewer has confirmed important fields |

Important items such as names, telephone numbers, amounts and reference numbers should have confirmation controls.

---

# 7. What the telephony provider should supply

The provider is not just supplying “a dialler.”

We should purchase the smallest telecom foundation that supplies these functions:

| Required telecom capability | Why needed |
|---|---|
| Indian business/DID number | Customer entry point |
| Inbound PSTN connectivity | Receive ordinary calls |
| Outbound PSTN connectivity | Call ordinary numbers |
| SIP or API call control | Let our software initiate/control calls |
| IVR primitives | Collect intent and route |
| Call-status webhooks | Know ringing, answered, failed, completed |
| Media streaming or recording | Process audio |
| DTMF capture | “Press 1, press 2” interactions |
| Caller-ID management | Legal and trusted display |
| Concurrency | Multiple calls simultaneously |
| CDR access | Audit, costing and compliance |
| Number masking | Protect worker/customer numbers |
| Failover | Maintain service |
| Consent/commercial-calling support | Outbound governance |

Exotel’s Voice APIs can make calls, construct IVR flows and manage routing programmatically. citeturn982098search3turn982098search43

Smartflo provides APIs, webhooks, virtual numbers, recording and CRM integration; its API Dialplan is expressly intended for custom workflows beyond the standard interface. citeturn982098search14turn982098search33turn982098search36

---

# 8. BPO/contact-centre feature glossary

Below are the terms we discussed earlier and what each one actually means.

## 8.1 DID / virtual number

A telephone number customers dial.

It may route to:

- IVR
- Queue
- Agent
- Callback workflow
- Voicebot
- External number

It is not merely a label; it is connected to a licensed telecom network.

---

## 8.2 IVR — Interactive Voice Response

An automated telephone menu.

Example:

```text
Press 1 for sales
Press 2 for support
Press 3 for appointment
Press 4 to request a callback
```

Modern IVR can also use speech:

> “Please briefly describe why you are calling.”

For our model, IVR is the first structured work-intake mechanism.

---

## 8.3 ACD — Automatic Call Distributor

ACD decides where an inbound call should go.

It can route based on:

- Queue
- Skill
- Language
- Priority
- Availability
- Customer type
- Working hours
- Previous agent
- Client
- SLA risk

Our orchestration product would add cross-client priority and flexible-workforce logic above the standard ACD.

---

## 8.4 Queue

A waiting list of calls or work items.

Queues may include:

- Tamil support
- English support
- Sales
- Complaints
- Fraud
- Client A
- Priority callbacks

Our system should ultimately use a unified work-item queue, but retain logical client and skill partitions.

---

## 8.5 Callback

Instead of keeping the customer waiting, the system records their place or creates a work item and calls them later.

Types:

- Queue callback
- Scheduled callback
- Automatic callback
- Agent-initiated callback
- Failed-call callback

This feature strongly suits the flexible women-workforce model.

---

## 8.6 Preview dialler

The agent sees the customer information first and chooses when to place the call.

Best for:

- Complex sales
- Collections
- Recruitment
- High-value accounts

It prioritises context over speed.

---

## 8.7 Click-to-call

The agent clicks a telephone icon in CRM.

The system:

1. Calls the agent
2. Calls the customer
3. Bridges both parties

Or it places the call directly through a WebRTC softphone.

Suitable for low- and medium-volume operations.

---

## 8.8 Power dialler

The system automatically calls the next lead when the agent finishes the previous call.

It normally dials one lead for one available agent.

Benefits:

- Less manual work
- Higher calling productivity
- Lower risk than predictive dialling

This is likely sufficient for the early outbound operation.

---

## 8.9 Progressive dialler

The system automatically dials when it knows an agent is ready and may use customer context and campaign rules.

It resembles power dialling but may have more sophisticated pacing and availability logic.

---

## 8.10 Predictive dialler

The platform predicts:

- Answer rates
- Call duration
- Agent availability
- Abandoned-call risk

It may dial several numbers before agents become free so that a connected customer is immediately assigned.

Benefits:

- Very high agent utilisation

Risks:

- Silent or abandoned calls
- Poor customer experience
- Regulatory issues
- Complex pacing
- Not suitable for low volumes
- Not aligned with personalised SME service initially

We do **not** need to build predictive dialling for the pilot.

---

## 8.11 Auto dialler / outbound dialler

A broad name for systems that dial numbers automatically.

It may mean:

- Preview
- Power
- Progressive
- Predictive
- Agentless recorded-message calls

Always ask the vendor which type they actually provide.

---

## 8.12 OBD — Outbound Dialling

Often used by Indian vendors for automated outbound calls.

It may support:

- Recorded announcements
- Payment reminders
- Appointment reminders
- Surveys
- Press-key responses
- Agent connection
- Campaign reports

Smartflo publicly offers outbound-dialling products and APIs, including recording, monitoring and CRM integration on relevant offerings. citeturn982098search45

---

## 8.13 Agentless outbound

The system calls the customer and plays an automated message or voicebot without a human agent.

Examples:

- “Your appointment is tomorrow”
- “Press 1 to confirm”
- Delivery notification
- Payment reminder

If the customer requests help, the system can create a callback task.

This could reduce manual workload significantly.

---

## 8.14 Answering-machine detection

The system tries to determine whether a person or voicemail answered.

Useful for international campaigns and large outbound operations.

It is not always accurate, especially across countries and network conditions.

---

## 8.15 Call bridging

The provider creates two call legs and connects them:

```text
Agent leg + Customer leg = One conversation
```

The two parties do not need to know each other’s personal number.

---

## 8.16 Number masking

The system shows a temporary or business number instead of the customer’s or employee’s personal number.

Used by:

- Delivery companies
- E-commerce platforms
- Taxi platforms
- Marketplaces

This is important for protecting home-based workers.

---

## 8.17 WebRTC softphone

A telephone interface inside the browser or application.

The agent uses:

- Headset
- Browser
- Internet connection

The call still reaches the telephone network through the telecom provider.

Our Flutter/web application can contain this interface if the provider offers suitable WebRTC/SIP SDKs.

---

## 8.18 SIP trunk

A SIP trunk connects our communication software or PBX to a telecom provider.

It is like a programmable telephone connection for many simultaneous calls.

It provides:

- Inbound and outbound voice paths
- Phone numbers
- Concurrency
- SIP signalling
- Audio/RTP media

For a mature custom platform, SIP trunking may be more flexible than using a vendor’s complete agent interface.

Exotel documents SIP-trunk integration for contact-centre and Voice AI platforms accessing Indian PSTN connectivity. citeturn982098search21

---

## 8.19 Media streaming

The provider sends live call audio to our application.

We use it for:

- Live transcript
- Sentiment
- Priority detection
- AI assistant
- Redaction
- Voice authentication
- Real-time QA

This is central to the product architecture you described.

---

## 8.20 Call recording

Captures the conversation.

Possible formats:

- Mixed mono: both speakers in one channel
- Dual-channel/stereo: one speaker per channel
- Provider recording
- Our media-stream recording

Dual-channel is better for:

- Diarisation
- QA
- Speaker-specific analytics
- Transcript accuracy

---

## 8.21 Whisper

A supervisor speaks to the agent without the customer hearing.

Use:

- Coaching
- Complex case guidance
- New-agent assistance

---

## 8.22 Barge-in

A supervisor joins the call and can speak to both parties.

Use:

- Escalation
- Complaint intervention
- Training
- Compliance

---

## 8.23 Silent monitoring

A supervisor listens without either party hearing.

Use:

- QA
- Training
- Live support

Recording and monitoring policies require appropriate disclosure and legal review.

---

## 8.24 Disposition

The structured outcome selected after a call.

Examples:

- No answer
- Busy
- Callback requested
- Interested
- Not interested
- Complaint
- Resolved
- Escalated
- Wrong number

Dispositions make reporting and automated next actions possible.

---

## 8.25 After-call work — ACW

Work completed after the customer disconnects:

- Notes
- Status
- Follow-up
- CRM update
- Email
- Escalation

ACW is genuine productive work and must be included in capacity and cost calculations.

---

## 8.26 AHT — Average Handling Time

Usually:

> Talk time + hold time + after-call work

It is used to forecast workforce and calculate unit cost.

For our operation, AHT must be measured separately by:

- Client
- Service
- Language
- Priority
- Worker skill
- Channel

---

## 8.27 Service level

A target such as:

> 80% of calls answered within 30 seconds.

For our callback model:

> 90% of P2 requests called back within 15 minutes.

Service levels determine the amount of buffer capacity required.

---

## 8.28 Abandonment rate

Percentage of customers who disconnect before being served.

Callback can reduce abandonment.

---

## 8.29 First-contact resolution — FCR

Percentage of issues solved during the first meaningful interaction without further contact.

High FCR generally indicates:

- Better knowledge
- Better authority
- Better handover
- Better systems

---

## 8.30 Skill-based routing

Routes work only to workers with required capabilities.

Examples:

- Tamil
- German
- Dental-client certification
- Complaint Level 2
- Recruitment screening
- Financial-data clearance

This will be part of our proprietary orchestration layer.

---

## 8.31 Workforce management — WFM

Includes:

- Demand forecasting
- Shift planning
- Capacity calculation
- Attendance
- Adherence
- Intraday reallocation
- Leave
- Scheduling
- Skills

Existing WFM products usually schedule employees for one organisation or contact centre. Our unique extension is flexible, shared, multi-client capacity.

---

## 8.32 Workforce optimisation — WFO

Broader than WFM.

It may include:

- Scheduling
- QA
- Coaching
- Performance
- Speech analytics
- Forecasting
- Employee engagement

---

## 8.33 Quality management — QM

Systematically evaluates customer interactions.

A scorecard may assess:

- Greeting
- Verification
- Accuracy
- Empathy
- Script compliance
- Notes
- Resolution
- Closure
- Data security

---

## 8.34 Speech analytics

Analyses recorded or live speech for:

- Keywords
- Sentiment
- Silence
- Interruptions
- Compliance phrases
- Escalation indicators
- Intent
- Agent behaviour

This can be built using transcription plus our own rules/AI.

---

## 8.35 Sentiment analysis

Estimates whether the customer is:

- Satisfied
- Neutral
- Frustrated
- Angry
- Distressed

It should inform priority but should not be trusted as the sole decision maker.

---

## 8.36 Screen pop

When a call arrives, the relevant customer record automatically opens.

In our system, the screen pop should show:

- Client identity
- Customer history
- Open issues
- Priority
- Script
- Knowledge
- Previous interactions

---

## 8.37 CTI — Computer Telephony Integration

Connects the telephone system with CRM/helpdesk/software.

Examples:

- Click-to-call in CRM
- Screen pop
- Automatic call logging
- Recording linked to customer
- Disposition update
- Callback creation

CTI is the bridge between the telecom layer and our operational product.

---

## 8.38 Omnichannel

Combines:

- Voice
- WhatsApp
- Email
- SMS
- Web chat
- Social media
- Video

A true omnichannel system preserves the same customer history across all channels.

---

## 8.39 SLA

A contractual service commitment:

- Response time
- Resolution time
- Availability
- Quality
- Accuracy
- Escalation
- Reporting

The priority engine should calculate the remaining SLA time for every work item.

---

# 9. Buy versus build matrix

## Phase-one recommendation

| Capability | Existing product initially | Build later? |
|---|---|---:|
| Indian phone number/PSTN | Exotel or Smartflo | No |
| SIP/media access | Telecom provider | No |
| Basic IVR | Provider | Possibly custom flow UI |
| Call recording fallback | Provider | Add our media recording |
| Live media stream | Provider API/SIP | Our processing service |
| Tamil transcription | Sarvam | Integration and correction UI |
| CRM | Zoho CRM | Replace only if justified |
| Helpdesk/customer history | Zoho Desk | Custom unified timeline later |
| Worker records | Zoho People | Custom fields/integration |
| Basic scheduling | Zoho Shifts/People | Custom capacity engine |
| Accounting | Zoho Books | No |
| Knowledge base | Zoho Desk/Google Workspace | Custom contextual delivery |
| Agent interface | Provider/Zoho initially | **Yes** |
| Unified work queue | Limited existing tools | **Yes** |
| Cross-client priority | Not adequately available | **Yes** |
| Availability matching | Partial | **Yes** |
| Capacity/sellability | Missing | **Yes** |
| Worker TEE/TEM earnings | Missing | **Yes** |
| Client-unit billing | Partial | **Yes** |
| AI/HITL learning | Partial | **Yes** |
| Client dashboard | Partial | **Yes** |

---

# 10. What percentage can our development absorb?

A sensible estimate by functional ownership is:

| Category | Share of long-term solution |
|---|---:|
| Telecom/carrier functions we must buy | 10%–15% |
| Commodity SaaS we can initially configure | 25%–35% |
| Integrations and data synchronisation we build | 15%–20% |
| Unique orchestration/product logic we build | 35%–50% |

Over time, some commodity functions can be absorbed into the product:

- CRM-lite
- Ticket history
- Knowledge
- QA
- Scheduling
- Reporting
- Billing

But there is no business advantage in immediately rebuilding Gmail, accounting, payroll or carrier telephony.

---

# 11. Cost model for the software architecture

## Lean five-worker pilot

| Component | Monthly planning range |
|---|---:|
| Google Workspace | ₹2,000–₹5,000 |
| Zoho CRM/Desk/People essential licences | ₹7,000–₹18,000 |
| Indian telephony licences/concurrency | ₹5,000–₹15,000 |
| Number and call usage | ₹5,000–₹20,000 |
| Sarvam transcription | ₹0.50/minute; e.g. 5,000 minutes = ₹2,500 |
| Cloud application/server/database | ₹3,000–₹12,000 |
| Object storage/backups | ₹1,000–₹5,000 |
| Monitoring/security/email delivery | ₹1,000–₹5,000 |
| **Estimated monthly total** | **₹24,000–₹80,500** |

Sarvam currently charges ₹30 per audio hour for speech-to-text and ₹45 per hour with diarisation. citeturn982098search27

---

## More custom architecture

If we replace much of Zoho CRM/Desk/People with our product:

| Cost | Effect |
|---|---|
| SaaS licence cost | Decreases |
| Cloud infrastructure | Increases modestly |
| Development responsibility | Increases |
| Support responsibility | Increases |
| Security responsibility | Increases |
| Backup/disaster recovery | Becomes ours |
| Product differentiation | Increases |
| Vendor dependency | Decreases |

For example, removing ₹15,000 of monthly SaaS licences does not necessarily save ₹15,000 if we then need:

- On-call support
- Database administration
- Security patching
- Backup validation
- Incident recovery
- Compliance evidence

Development can absorb licensing, but not operational responsibility.

---

# 12. Recommended technical evolution

## Stage 1 — Integrate before replacing

Use:

- Exotel/Smartflo
- Sarvam
- Zoho CRM/Desk/People
- Google Workspace

Build:

- Unified work-item service
- Priority service
- Capacity service
- Worker-matching service
- Earnings service
- Agent console

This proves the unique idea.

---

## Stage 2 — Replace expensive/limiting SaaS modules

After real use, determine whether to replace:

- Zoho Desk ticket layer
- Zoho CRM campaign layer
- Zoho Shifts
- Knowledge delivery
- QA
- Client dashboards

Do not replace them merely because coding is possible.

Replace only where:

- Licence cost is materially high
- Integration blocks the product
- User experience is poor
- Required data model is impossible
- Vendor lock-in threatens scale

---

## Stage 3 — Keep telecom interchangeable

Create a **Telephony Adapter Interface**:

```text
TelephonyProvider
├── initiate_call()
├── answer_call()
├── transfer_call()
├── start_stream()
├── stop_stream()
├── get_recording()
├── receive_event()
├── send_dtmf()
└── get_call_cost()
```

Then implement adapters:

- ExotelAdapter
- SmartfloAdapter
- TwilioAdapter
- AmazonConnectAdapter
- FutureProviderAdapter

This protects the product from being locked to one telecom provider.

---

# 13. Why the previous product review may not have identified all this

There are two likely reasons.

## First: the business model was not yet fully articulated

Earlier, the product may have been reviewed as:

- Women empowerment system
- Task management
- Workforce platform
- Productivity or service operation

This page has now introduced much more specific requirements:

- Multi-client pooled workers
- Weekly variable availability
- Real-time media
- Callback-first inbound
- Emergency prioritisation
- Task pre-emption
- Skill/language matching
- Telecom integration
- Client-capacity gating
- Reserved emergency capacity
- TEE-to-TEM worker compensation
- Unit-based client billing
- Live transcription
- Human-reviewed AI learning

A reviewer cannot reliably demand architecture for requirements that were not yet explicit.

## Second: code review and product-market architecture review are different

A code review may correctly assess:

- APIs
- Security
- tests
- database
- UI
- deployment
- architecture quality

while still missing:

- Contact-centre operating features
- Telecom compliance
- BPO pricing
- WFM
- Real-time audio
- SLA and queue theory
- Multi-client orchestration

The next review should be conducted against the **new consolidated requirements**, not the earlier product description.

---

# 14. The immediate next architectural deliverable

Before touching the existing code, create a **Functional Absorption Matrix** with these columns:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Capability | What the system must do |
| Business reason | Why it exists |
| Current product | Existing software candidate |
| Existing coverage % | How much it solves |
| API available | Integration feasibility |
| Monthly cost | Current external cost |
| Build complexity | Low/medium/high |
| Regulatory dependency | Whether we can own it |
| Build now/later/never | Decision |
| Existing code reuse | Relevant module |
| Acceptance criteria | Proof it works |
| Test requirement | Unit/API/Playwright/load/security |

The first technical proof should be only this end-to-end path:

```text
Customer calls Indian number
→ IVR captures reason
→ live audio reaches our server
→ Tamil transcript appears
→ work item is created
→ rule/AI assigns priority
→ suitable available worker is selected
→ worker sees customer context
→ callback is placed
→ notes and recording are stored
→ QA and billing records are produced
```

Once that works, we will know exactly which portions of Exotel, Smartflo, Sarvam and Zoho we genuinely need—and which portions our product can absorb.

### 0065 · CHATGPT · 2026-07-17T19:44:44Z

# 17/07/2026 22:52 — From BPO Platform to Distributed Work OS

Yes, partner. The direction is now much bigger than **Ival to the Able as a BPO company**.

Ival to the Able can become:

> **The first operating client and service partner of a separate SaaS platform that converts work into structured tasks, distributes it to qualified people or small teams, tracks delivery, preserves knowledge, calculates payment, and manages quality.**

That means the relationship can eventually become:

```text
Platform Company
      ↓
Provides SaaS + orchestration infrastructure
      ↓
Ival to the Able
      ↓
Runs women-empowerment services using the platform
      ↓
Serves its own clients and recruits partner teams
```

Ival to the Able does not need to own the software company. It can become:

- The first operational customer
- The first implementation partner
- A service reseller
- A channel partner
- A workforce supplier
- A testing and validation partner

This is much safer than mixing the software intellectual property, client operations, worker payments and social-enterprise ownership into one company from the beginning.

Your existing planning already says the business should move away from merely “selling people” and towards managed operations with SOPs, SLAs, quality, reports and measurable outcomes. fileciteturn1file1 The present idea extends that principle beyond BPO.

---

# 1. The product is not limited to BPO

The product can be described as:

## **Distributed Work Orchestration Platform**

It takes any piece of work and converts it into:

- A defined work item
- Required skills
- Priority
- Due date
- Estimated effort
- Available budget
- Knowledge required
- Evidence required
- Quality checks
- Payment rule
- Client billing rule
- Ownership and escalation

Then it allocates that work to:

- An individual
- A freelancer
- A part-time employee
- A women’s workforce
- A five-person micro-company
- A specialist partner
- An external service organisation
- An automated software agent
- A human-plus-AI team

The core principle is:

> **Work belongs to the platform and process—not permanently to a particular employee.**

---

# 2. Three different business entities can coexist

## Entity 1 — SaaS product company

Owns:

- Software
- Architecture
- Algorithms
- Integrations
- Product roadmap
- Licensing
- Platform support
- Intellectual property

Earns through:

- SaaS subscription
- Usage fees
- Transaction charges
- Integration fees
- Premium modules
- White-label licences

---

## Entity 2 — Ival to the Able social enterprise

Owns:

- Women-worker network
- Recruitment
- Training
- Social-impact mission
- Client delivery
- Operational supervision
- Local-market relationships

Earns through:

- Managed service fees
- Work-unit fees
- SLA fees
- Outcome incentives
- Client transition and training fees

---

## Entity 3 — Independent partner companies

Example:

> Five women form their own registered company.

They can:

- Use the platform
- Receive work from Ival to the Able
- Bring their own clients
- Use shared tools
- Pay platform subscription
- Pay marketplace/service commission
- Retain independence
- Build their own workforce

This creates a network instead of one central employer becoming a bottleneck.

---

# 3. How Ival to the Able can become a network platform

The future model could be:

```text
Clients
   ↓
Platform Marketplace / Service Exchange
   ↓
────────────────────────────────────────
Ival to the Able central team
Women’s micro-enterprise A
Freelancer B
Testing company C
Marketing team D
Regional-language team E
Software-development team F
────────────────────────────────────────
   ↓
Tasks completed, checked and billed
```

Ival to the Able may initially control all delivery.

Later it can become:

- Lead generator
- Quality certifier
- Process designer
- Training provider
- Client relationship owner
- Payment settlement layer
- Platform reseller
- Marketplace operator

So yes, Ival to the Able can eventually be “hacked” or unbundled—in a positive sense.

It does not need to employ everybody directly.

It can create a commercial ecosystem.

---

# 4. Types of work the application can support

Almost any work can use the platform when it can be converted into a measurable task.

## A. BPO and customer operations

| Work category | Example work items |
|---|---|
| Outbound calling | Call lead, qualify, schedule follow-up |
| Inbound callback | Review request, call customer, resolve |
| Customer support | Investigate ticket, respond, close |
| WhatsApp support | Answer enquiry, escalate, update status |
| Email support | Classify, respond and record outcome |
| Order support | Confirm, modify, cancel or escalate |
| Complaint handling | Collect facts, resolve or transfer |
| Collections | Reminder, promise-to-pay and follow-up |
| Survey work | Conduct questionnaire and submit result |

---

## B. Software development

| Area | Possible work items |
|---|---|
| Requirements | Write user story, clarify acceptance criteria |
| Architecture | Review component design |
| Backend | Build one endpoint or service |
| Frontend | Implement one screen/component |
| Database | Create migration or optimise query |
| Testing | Write unit tests |
| API testing | Validate endpoint scenarios |
| Playwright | Automate one business flow |
| Security | Review authentication or permissions |
| Code review | Review pull request |
| Documentation | Create developer or API document |
| Deployment | Configure pipeline |
| Production support | Investigate incident |
| Technical debt | Refactor defined module |

Software development fits particularly well because Git commits, pull requests, automated tests and deployments provide objective evidence.

---

## C. Product testing

| Testing type | Work item |
|---|---|
| Functional testing | Execute test scenario |
| Usability testing | Complete journey and record problems |
| Accessibility testing | Validate defined WCAG checks |
| Mobile testing | Test device/OS combination |
| Browser testing | Test Chrome, Edge, Safari scenario |
| Hardware testing | Run test protocol |
| Consumer product testing | Use product and submit structured review |
| Packaging testing | Inspect and document defects |
| Regression | Re-run approved suite |
| Exploratory testing | Investigate feature and log findings |

---

## D. Data and AI work

| Area | Work item |
|---|---|
| Data entry | Enter and validate record |
| Data cleaning | Correct defined errors |
| Labelling | Tag image, text, call or video |
| Transcription | Transcribe audio |
| Transcript verification | Correct AI transcript |
| Translation | Translate segment |
| AI evaluation | Score AI response |
| Prompt testing | Execute prompt suite |
| Dataset review | Validate sample |
| Document extraction | Extract structured fields |
| Duplicate detection | Review suspected duplicate |
| Research | Find and verify specified information |

---

## E. Marketing

| Area | Work item |
|---|---|
| Lead generation | Identify qualified organisation/contact |
| Outreach | Send approved email/message |
| Campaign operations | Configure and monitor campaign |
| Social posting | Prepare and schedule post |
| Comment moderation | Review and respond |
| Competitor research | Collect competitor data |
| SEO research | Analyse keywords |
| Listing management | Update business listings |
| Review management | Respond to reviews |
| Event outreach | Invite and track participants |
| Referral campaigns | Contact and follow up |

---

## F. Content creation

| Area | Work item |
|---|---|
| Blog | Research, draft, edit and publish |
| YouTube | Script, record, edit, thumbnail, upload |
| Podcast | Edit, transcribe and publish |
| Short video | Create reel/short |
| Social content | Design and caption |
| Newsletter | Draft, format and schedule |
| Presentation | Build slides |
| Brochure | Write and design |
| Product listing | Create title, copy and images |
| Translation/localisation | Adapt content by language |
| Content QA | Verify facts, grammar and brand rules |

A YouTube video itself becomes a workflow:

```text
Topic approval
→ research
→ script
→ review
→ recording
→ editing
→ subtitle
→ thumbnail
→ upload
→ metadata
→ quality check
→ analytics review
```

Different people can complete each stage.

---

## G. Recruitment and HR

| Area | Work item |
|---|---|
| Candidate sourcing | Find candidates |
| Resume screening | Assess against rules |
| Candidate calling | Verify availability |
| Screening interview | Complete questionnaire |
| Interview scheduling | Coordinate calendars |
| Reference checks | Contact references |
| Onboarding | Collect and validate documents |
| Attendance review | Check anomalies |
| Payroll inputs | Validate monthly data |
| Training administration | Assign and track learning |
| Employee support | Resolve HR query |

---

## H. Finance and accounting operations

| Area | Work item |
|---|---|
| Invoice entry | Record invoice |
| Invoice validation | Match order and receipt |
| Expense review | Check expense evidence |
| Reconciliation | Match bank transactions |
| Collections follow-up | Contact debtor |
| Supplier onboarding | Verify information |
| Payroll validation | Review changes |
| Management reporting | Prepare report |
| Tax-document collection | Collect required records |
| Audit preparation | Organise evidence |

Regulated decisions should remain with qualified professionals, but administrative work can be distributed.

---

## I. E-commerce

| Area | Work item |
|---|---|
| Product upload | Create SKU listing |
| Catalogue correction | Fix attributes |
| Order confirmation | Verify customer/order |
| Delivery follow-up | Check failed delivery |
| Returns | Validate request |
| Refund support | Prepare/referral workflow |
| Seller support | Answer marketplace issue |
| Inventory update | Update records |
| Review response | Respond using policy |
| Image QA | Check product images |

---

## J. Education

| Area | Work item |
|---|---|
| Admission enquiry | Respond and qualify |
| Student follow-up | Contact student/parent |
| Application checking | Verify documents |
| Class scheduling | Coordinate timetable |
| Assignment review | Administrative verification |
| Learning support | Answer non-academic queries |
| Attendance follow-up | Contact absentees |
| Content digitisation | Convert material |
| Translation | Localise learning content |
| Examination support | Schedule and communicate |

---

## K. Healthcare administration

Suitable non-clinical work includes:

- Appointment booking
- Reminder calls
- Patient registration
- Insurance-document collection
- Report-delivery coordination
- Feedback
- Follow-up scheduling
- Medical-record indexing
- Billing support

Clinical judgement and emergencies must remain with qualified healthcare providers.

---

## L. Legal administration

| Work | Example |
|---|---|
| Case tracking | Update hearing dates/status |
| Document indexing | Categorise legal documents |
| Deadline monitoring | Create reminders |
| Client follow-up | Collect documents |
| Chronology | Prepare event timeline |
| Evidence catalogue | Record source and metadata |
| Transcription | Convert meeting/audio |
| Billing administration | Prepare time records |

The structured case sheets already in your files demonstrate that legal work can be divided into status, documents, questions, action items, risks and hearing preparation. fileciteturn1file18

---

## M. Field and physical work

Even offline work can be supported where evidence is captured.

Examples:

- Store audit
- Site inspection
- Delivery verification
- Stock counting
- Photograph collection
- Equipment check
- Event staffing
- Survey visit
- Maintenance inspection
- Document pickup

Each task can require:

- Location or site
- Time window
- Checklist
- Photograph
- Signature
- Supervisor approval

The system should avoid covert tracking. Workers actively submit evidence.

---

# 5. Work must be decomposable before it enters the platform

A task must answer:

| Field | Question |
|---|---|
| Objective | What must be achieved? |
| Input | What information/material is provided? |
| Skill | Who is qualified? |
| Time | How long is expected? |
| Deadline | When must it be completed? |
| Priority | How urgent is it? |
| Dependency | What must happen first? |
| Output | What must be produced? |
| Evidence | How will completion be proved? |
| QA | How will quality be measured? |
| Price | What does the client pay? |
| Worker earning | What does the worker receive? |
| Escalation | What happens if blocked? |

Without this structure, the platform becomes another generic task manager.

---

# 6. What differentiates this from Jira, Upwork or Zoho Projects?

| Platform type | Main model | Missing compared with your concept |
|---|---|---|
| Jira | Project/team task tracking | Worker marketplace, capacity selling, payment |
| Upwork | Client hires freelancer | Managed workflow, pooled delivery and standard QA |
| Uber | Assign local job to available provider | Knowledge work, multi-stage workflow and client history |
| Zoho People | Manage employees | Cross-company task marketplace and client billing |
| BPO WFM | Schedule call-centre agents | Independent micro-companies and cross-sector work |
| CRM | Manage customer pipeline | Workforce allocation and delivery economics |
| Helpdesk | Manage support cases | Flexible supply, worker payment and service marketplace |
| Your platform | Convert demand into governed distributed execution | Integrates all layers |

The differentiation is not just task creation.

It is:

> **Demand-to-delivery-to-payment orchestration across organisational boundaries.**

---

# 7. Possible participant models

## Model A — Individual worker

- Works part-time
- Selects availability
- Receives certified tasks
- Gets paid through platform rules

## Model B — Managed employee

- Belongs to Ival to the Able
- Works under its supervision
- Uses the platform for allocation and evidence

## Model C — Freelancer

- Independent
- Accepts task/project
- Paid per service unit or milestone

## Model D — Micro-enterprise

- Five-person registered company
- Receives a service package
- Allocates work internally
- Responsible for agreed output

## Model E — Specialist partner

- Translation agency
- Testing company
- Accounting team
- Content studio
- Legal-process provider

## Model F — Automation/AI agent

- Completes repeatable parts
- Human verifies exceptions
- Platform records machine cost and human cost separately

---

# 8. Ival to the Able’s strategic choice

Subha may continue building the company using the older approach:

> Find client → train agent → allocate agent → charge by person.

Let that work continue without disrupting it prematurely.

Meanwhile, the platform can be designed ahead of the current operation.

Then the migration path becomes:

## Stage 1 — Observe

- Collect actual workflow
- Track current methods
- Identify pain
- Record current costs
- Do not force architecture prematurely

## Stage 2 — Assist

- Introduce task records
- Add call history
- Add scheduling
- Add reporting
- Keep current service model

## Stage 3 — Orchestrate

- Move from named agents to queues
- Add shared knowledge
- Add flexible allocation
- Add quality and payment engines

## Stage 4 — Productise

- Ival to the Able becomes a SaaS customer
- Its workers use the platform
- It resells to other teams

## Stage 5 — Ecosystem

- Independent teams register
- Clients purchase capacity
- Ival to the Able earns service/referral revenue
- Platform earns SaaS/usage revenue

---

# 9. Do not describe it as “Ival to the Able can be hacked”

The strategic term should be:

## **Federated delivery model**

or

## **Partner-led service network**

or

## **Micro-enterprise operating network**

The company is not broken apart.

Instead, execution is decentralised while standards, technology, client governance and quality remain connected.

---

# 10. Major advantages

## For women workers

- Flexible participation
- Visible relationship between skill and earnings
- Opportunity to progress from individual to business owner
- Work history and capability profile
- Training pathways
- Less dependence on one employer
- Ability to join different service categories

## For small businesses

- Avoid hiring full-time staff
- Buy only required capacity
- Use enterprise-style processes
- Access multiple skills
- Scale gradually

## For Ival to the Able

- Expand without directly employing everyone
- Serve more clients
- Standardise delivery
- Retain client relationship
- Create recurring platform/reseller income

## For the SaaS company

- Real operational validation
- Unique training data
- Multiple verticals
- Network effects
- Recurring revenue
- Less dependence on one service business

---

# 11. Major risks

| Risk | Why serious |
|---|---|
| Over-expansion | Supporting every work category creates an unfocused product |
| Worker classification | Employee, contractor and partner legal status differs |
| Quality inconsistency | Distributed teams may deliver unevenly |
| Client data exposure | Many parties access data |
| Marketplace leakage | Client and worker may bypass platform |
| Payment disputes | Task completion may be subjective |
| Complexity | Too many workflows and pricing methods |
| Product-service conflict | SaaS and BPO priorities may compete |
| Social mission dilution | Commercial pressure may reduce worker welfare |
| Governance | Who is responsible when a partner fails? |

---

# 12. Correct initial product boundary

Although the platform can eventually support many industries, do not build every vertical initially.

The first product should support only work with these characteristics:

- Digital or digitally recordable
- Clearly decomposable
- Moderate skill
- Measurable outcome
- Non-emergency
- Low regulatory risk
- Suitable for flexible workers
- Easy QA
- Repeatable across clients

## Recommended initial verticals

1. Outbound and callback operations  
2. Recruitment screening  
3. Data and catalogue operations  
4. Content and social-media operations  
5. Software testing  

This gives variety while still using the same core engine.

---

# 13. The core product modules

| Module | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Organisation and tenant management | Companies and teams |
| Worker profile | Skills, language, certifications |
| Availability and capacity | Time supply |
| Service catalogue | Standard work products |
| Work-item engine | Tasks and dependencies |
| Priority/SLA engine | Urgency and due times |
| Matching engine | Best worker/team |
| Knowledge and instructions | Context |
| Evidence and QA | Completion proof |
| Earnings engine | Worker/payment calculation |
| Billing engine | Client charges |
| Partner management | Freelancers and micro-enterprises |
| Client portal | Requests and reporting |
| Integration hub | CRM, dialler, Git, content systems |
| Audit and compliance | History and accountability |
| Analytics | Capacity, quality and profitability |

---

# 14. The fundamental product hierarchy

```text
Platform
   ↓
Organisation
   ↓
Service Catalogue
   ↓
Client Contract
   ↓
Work Order
   ↓
Work Items
   ↓
Worker / Team / Partner
   ↓
Evidence
   ↓
Quality Approval
   ↓
Client Billing
   ↓
Worker or Partner Payment
```

That hierarchy can support BPO, software, testing, marketing, content, data and many other services without redesigning the whole system.

---

# Final conclusion

Your thought is no longer simply:

> “How can Ival to the Able employ home-based women?”

It has evolved into:

> **How can independent people, women’s groups, freelancers and small companies combine their fragmented time and skills into reliable, measurable enterprise services?**

Ival to the Able can become the first operating network that proves it.

The separate SaaS product can become the infrastructure that allows the model to spread beyond one founder, one company, one industry and one location.

You are not “going here and there” without value. You have been doing **founder-led problem discovery**: repeatedly testing the idea from business, workforce, pricing, client, technology, social-impact and product angles.

However, the discussion is now at the point where further unstructured brainstorming will create duplication. It must be converted into a controlled product baseline.

The current brochure still describes Ival Puthiyaval as a conventional office-style BPO with 24/7 worldwide support, 50+ trained professionals and standard BPO services. That does not yet represent the flexible, home-based, multi-client women-workforce model developed in this discussion.  The previous meeting record also shows that costing, documentation, ownership and standardisation were already recognised as gaps. 

---

# 1. What stage is this?

## Current stage: **Late Discovery / Concept Definition**

This is **before formal product architecture** and before an MVP build.

| Stage | Status | Meaning |
|---|---:|---|
| Problem discovery | **Strongly underway** | You have identified genuine operational problems |
| Founder brainstorming | **Advanced** | Many ideas and edge cases have been explored |
| Business-model hypothesis | **Emerging** | Service company + SaaS product + partner network |
| Product definition | **Partially formed** | Core modules are visible but not frozen |
| Market validation | **Insufficient** | No structured customer interviews or paid pilot evidence yet |
| Requirements baseline | **Not complete** | Ideas exist across conversation, not one controlled document |
| Architecture | **Not approved** | Technology options discussed, but no signed architecture decision |
| Prototype | **Not started for this exact model** | Existing code may be reusable, but it is not yet assessed against this requirement |
| MVP | **Not started** | No frozen scope or acceptance criteria |
| Product-market fit | **Not applicable yet** | Requires usage and paying customers |

## More precise description

You are at:

> **Discovery → synthesis → architecture-baseline transition**

The next move is **not another broad brainstorming round** and **not immediate full development**.

The next move is:

1. Freeze what problem is being solved.
2. Separate service-company requirements from SaaS-product requirements.
3. Create a capability inventory.
4. Decide what must be bought, configured, integrated and built.
5. Build one end-to-end proof.
6. Validate it with Ival Puthiyaval and one real client.

---

# 2. What exactly is being created?

There are **three connected businesses**, not one.

## A. Ival Puthiyaval — social-enterprise service operator

It recruits and supports women, acquires clients, delivers managed work, trains workers and maintains service quality.

## B. Workforce Orchestration SaaS

It converts demand into prioritised work, matches it to available qualified capacity, tracks execution, preserves knowledge and calculates billing and earnings.

## C. Partner delivery network

Freelancers, women’s groups and small companies may use the platform to perform work for Ival Puthiyaval or their own clients.

These must be designed separately even if the same people initially operate all three.

---

# 3. Architecture principle

The critical principle is:

> **Buy regulated infrastructure. Reuse commodity business software selectively. Build the unique orchestration intelligence.**

Do not rebuild:

- Telecom carrier connectivity
- Public telephone numbers
- Email infrastructure
- Accounting/GST engine
- Commodity cloud storage
- Basic identity standards

Build:

- Capacity model
- Sellable-capacity engine
- Cross-client queue
- Priority engine
- Worker matching
- Knowledge-aware assignment
- Service-unit costing
- Worker TEE-to-TEM calculation
- Client pricing and profitability
- Human-reviewed AI learning
- Partner-company workflow

---

# 5. Detailed explanation of each architecture number

## [1] Client and customer channels

This is how work enters the system:

- Indian inbound telephone number
- Outbound campaign
- WhatsApp
- Email
- Web form
- Client API
- Excel/CSV batch
- Client portal
- Social-media message
- Future mobile app

Every channel must eventually create the same basic object:

> **Work Item**

A work item contains client, customer, service, channel, priority, SLA, required skill, expected effort, value, evidence and status.

### Build or buy?

- Email infrastructure: buy
- Telephone connectivity: buy
- WhatsApp connectivity: buy from authorised platform/BSP
- Intake adapters and normalisation: build

---

## [2] Regulated communication gateway

This is one of the few components that **must always remain with an authorised external provider**.

It supplies:

- Indian telephone number
- PSTN/mobile connectivity
- Caller ID
- Incoming and outgoing call legs
- IVR primitives
- DTMF
- Call-status events
- Concurrency
- Call-detail records
- Recording or media stream
- Number masking
- Telecom compliance interface

### Recommended initial decision

Evaluate only:

1. **Tata Smartflo**
2. **Exotel**

Tata publicly lists:

- Smartflo ITS at ₹1,000 per licence/month
- Smartflo Pro at ₹1,250 per licence/month
- Outbound Dialler Unlimited at ₹1,200 per concurrency/month
- Voice Streaming inbound/outbound at ₹1,100 per concurrency/month
- Setup fees from ₹10,000 or ₹20,000 depending on product
- Its unlimited plans are subject to a pooled 5,000-minute fair-usage policy per user/month. 

Exotel publicly lists small-business packages at ₹9,999, ₹19,999 and ₹49,499 with different validity, credits, virtual numbers and agent allowances. 

### Why 100% mandatory?

Because software development cannot legally manufacture an Indian business number or independently connect to the Indian public telephone network.

### How long will it be used?

**Permanently**, although the provider should be replaceable.

Create a `TelephonyProvider` interface so Exotel can later be replaced by Tata, Airtel IQ, Amazon Connect with an India carrier arrangement, or another authorised provider.

---

## [3] Omnichannel intake and normalisation

This converts each incoming event into a standard structure.

Example:

```json
{
  "client": "ABC Dental",
  "channel": "voice",
  "customer": "Priya",
  "intent": "appointment_change",
  "priority": "P3",
  "sla_due_at": "2026-07-18T11:30:00+05:30",
  "required_language": "ta-IN",
  "required_skill": "dental_appointments"
}
```

### Build or buy?

**Build.**

Existing tools can each create tickets, leads or calls, but the unique value is converting all channels and all clients into one governed work-item model.

### Priority

**P0 — first product module.**

Without this, the rest remains disconnected software.

---

## [4] Speech, AI and human decision layer

Functions:

- Streaming Tamil/Tamil-English transcription
- Speaker separation
- Transcript correction
- Summary
- Intent classification
- Entity extraction
- Priority recommendation
- Relevant knowledge retrieval
- Human override
- Learning from approved corrections

### Initial provider

**Sarvam STT adapter**

Current published pricing:

- Speech-to-text: ₹30/hour = ₹0.50/minute
- With diarisation: ₹45/hour = ₹0.75/minute
- Billing is per second. 

### Why use Sarvam initially?

Because your actual workload will include:

- Tamil
- Tamil-English mixing
- Regional accents
- Telephone-quality audio
- Indian names and addresses

### Is Sarvam permanently mandatory?

No. **Speech-to-text capability is mandatory; Sarvam is the preferred initial engine.**

Build an abstraction:

```text
SpeechProvider
├── SarvamAdapter
├── OpenAIAdapter
├── GoogleAdapter
└── FutureAdapter
```

Benchmark all providers using real recordings.

### Priority

- Batch transcription: **P0**
- Live partial transcript: **P1**
- Real-time urgency detection: **P2**, after accuracy is proved

---

## [5] Work-item, priority and SLA engine

This is part of the intellectual property.

It determines:

- Is this a task, enquiry, complaint or emergency?
- What priority?
- What response deadline?
- Can another task be interrupted?
- Which client rule applies?
- Does a human need to approve?
- What escalation path applies?
- What happens when the SLA is at risk?

### Priority

**P0 — mandatory core.**

### Why not use only Zoho Desk/Jira?

They can hold tickets and priorities, but they do not natively implement your complete multi-client, flexible-capacity, pre-emption and commercial logic.

---

## [6] Knowledge and customer-state layer

This prevents knowledge from remaining inside one worker’s head.

It contains:

- Customer timeline
- Previous calls/messages
- Open issues
- Client SOP
- Scripts
- FAQs
- Policies
- Escalation contacts
- Product information
- Version history
- Worker notes
- AI-generated summary
- Human-verified facts

### Initial implementation

Use your own PostgreSQL domain model and object storage.

Zoho Desk may be used during a short service pilot, but it should not become the long-term system of record if the orchestration product is being built immediately.

### Priority

**P0.**

Handover is impossible without it.

---

## [7] Workforce capacity engine

This stores:

- Preferred availability
- Confirmed weekly availability
- Committed service blocks
- Skills
- Languages
- Certifications
- Reliability
- QA score
- Current state
- Reserved capacity
- Internal cost
- Maximum work block
- Device/internet readiness

It calculates:

- Gross capacity
- Reliable capacity
- Buffer
- Already sold capacity
- Remaining sellable capacity
- Skill gaps
- Time-window gaps

### Initial implementation

Build this in the product rather than purchasing an enterprise WFM platform.

### Temporary tool

Google Forms/Sheets may be used only for collecting the first availability dataset while the module is built.

### Priority

**P0 — the strongest differentiator.**

---

## [8] Matching, allocation and control tower

This decides:

- Which worker should receive the work?
- Which partner company can perform it?
- Should an urgent case use reserved capacity?
- Can a low-priority outbound task be paused?
- Is the worker certified for the client?
- Is handover safe?
- Is the assignment profitable?
- Will accepting this work break another SLA?

### Priority

- Basic rules-based assignment: **P0**
- Dynamic pre-emption: **P1**
- AI optimisation: **P2**
- Forecast optimisation: **P2/P3**

Do not start with AI optimisation. Start with transparent rules.

---

## [9] Worker and partner execution console

The worker sees:

- Assigned work
- Client identity
- Customer history
- Script
- Knowledge
- Live/final transcript
- Suggested priority
- Due time
- Call/chat controls
- Notes
- Required evidence
- Handover
- Earnings impact

### Technology

- Flutter for mobile/desktop where appropriate
- Web console for supervisors and power users
- FastAPI backend

### Priority

**P0 for a basic console.**

A polished omnichannel console is later.

---

## [10] Quality, security and audit

Mandatory functions:

- Tenant/client separation
- Role-based access
- MFA
- Consent status
- Recording access control
- Immutable event history
- QA sampling
- Corrections
- Dispute handling
- Data retention
- Worker exit/access revocation
- Critical-field verification
- AI decision audit

### Priority

Security and audit foundations are **P0**, not a later “enterprise feature.”

Advanced compliance certifications are later.

---

## [11] Commercial engine

This converts TEE into TEM.

### Worker side

- Reserved-time payment
- Active-work payment
- Unit incentive
- Skill premium
- Language premium
- Quality bonus
- Reliability bonus
- Training recovery
- Partner-company settlement

### Client side

- Platform fee
- Service units
- Coverage
- SLA premium
- Software allocation
- Telecom usage
- AI usage
- Setup
- Overage
- Outcome incentive

### Priority

- Basic costing and profitability: **P0**
- Automated invoicing and settlement: **P1**
- Marketplace wallet: **P3**

---

## [12] Portals, reporting and learning loop

### Client portal

- Submit work
- View queue
- View SLA
- View results
- View recordings subject to permission
- Approve exceptions
- Download reports
- See charges

### Worker portal

- Availability
- Assignments
- Training
- Quality
- Earnings
- Certifications

### Learning loop

Unknown case:

1. Rule fails.
2. AI proposes classification.
3. Human approves/corrects.
4. Decision is audited.
5. Rule owner reviews it.
6. Approved rule or knowledge is published.

### Priority

- Basic dashboard: **P1**
- Client self-service portal: **P2**
- Automated learning governance: **P2**

---

## [13] Platform foundation

Recommended foundation:

- **GitHub**: source, issues, pull requests, actions
- **FastAPI**: backend services
- **PostgreSQL**: transactional source of truth
- **Redis**: caching, locks and short-lived state
- **Message broker**: NATS/RabbitMQ/Kafka depending on proven demand
- **S3-compatible object storage**: audio, evidence and documents
- **Flutter**: worker application
- **Web application**: admin/client/control tower
- **OpenTelemetry**: traces, metrics and logs
- **Docker**: reproducible deployment
- **Playwright**: end-to-end tests
- **Pytest**: backend tests
- **GitHub Actions**: CI/CD
- **Architecture Decision Records**: major technical decisions
- **Sprint documents**: context and delivery memory

### Priority

All core engineering controls are **P0 from day one**.

---

# 6. Tools that are 100% mandatory

“100% mandatory” should refer to a **capability**, not necessarily one permanent vendor.

| Mandatory capability | Initial tool | Why mandatory | Expected duration |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source control | Git + GitHub | Audit, rollback, collaboration, release traceability | Permanent |
| Sprint/issue control | GitHub Issues/Projects + repository MDs | AI context cannot be project memory | Permanent |
| Company identity/email | Google Workspace or Zoho Workplace | Domain email, identity, controlled documents | Permanent; vendor replaceable |
| Telecom/PSTN | Exotel or Tata Smartflo | Indian number and legal network connectivity | Permanent category |
| Transaction database | PostgreSQL | Core source of truth | Permanent |
| Audio/evidence storage | S3-compatible storage | Recording and evidence retention | Permanent |
| Automated testing | Pytest + Playwright | Protect rapid AI-generated development | Permanent |
| CI/CD | GitHub Actions | Repeatable, auditable releases | Permanent |
| Monitoring/audit | OpenTelemetry + logging/monitoring backend | Production trust | Permanent |
| Backup/recovery | Automated database/object backups | Business survival | Permanent |
| STT capability | Sarvam initially | Tamil/Tamil-English workflow | Permanent category |
| Accounting | Zoho Books or equivalent | GST, invoicing and financial records | Permanent category |
| MFA/password management | Identity provider + password vault | Remote workforce security | Permanent |

---

# 7. Tools I would not prioritise now

| Tool/product | Decision | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Salesforce | Do not buy now | Expensive and duplicates the product domain |
| Zendesk | Do not buy now | Ticketing can be modelled in the product |
| NICE/Verint WFM | Do not buy now | Enterprise scale before proven workload |
| Genesys/Five9 | Do not buy now | Excessive platform scope for pilot |
| Amazon Connect | Research adapter, not initial India carrier | Powerful platform but India telephony design needs provider validation |
| Microsoft Dynamics Contact Center | Not now | High enterprise complexity |
| Predictive dialler | Not now | Early volumes do not justify it |
| Full AI autonomous routing | Not now | Rules and HITL must be proven first |
| Custom payroll | Do not build | Use compliant accounting/payroll products |
| Custom email/calendar | Never build | Commodity infrastructure |
| Blockchain/payment token | Not relevant | No validated need |

---

# 8. Why Google or Zoho?

## Google Workspace

Use it for:

- Domain email
- Docs
- Drive
- Meet
- Calendar
- Founder and management collaboration

Do not use it as the product database or workflow engine.

## Zoho

Zoho remains valuable for commodity back-office functions:

- Zoho Books
- Possibly Payroll
- Possibly Forms during pilot
- Possibly Sign
- Possibly Campaigns/Social for the service company

Zoho Creator should be used only if it accelerates a disposable operational prototype. Since you have Codex-assisted FastAPI/Flutter capability and want a SaaS product, Creator should **not automatically become the core product architecture**.

Zoho People can be free for up to five users and offers per-user HR capabilities, but your flexible worker and cross-client capacity model would still require substantial custom logic. 

---

# 9. Cost baseline

## A. One-time initial costs

| Item | Indicative cost |
|---|---:|
| Domain/branding/legal setup | Separate business budget |
| Tata Smartflo setup | ₹10,000–₹20,000 depending on product |
| Initial devices/headsets | Based on worker model |
| Security/configuration | Mostly internal effort initially |
| Product development | Primarily AI-assisted founder/Codex effort |
| Vendor integration proof | Development effort plus API usage |

Tata’s current published setup charges and monthly rentals are shown on its official CCaaS page. 

## B. Five-worker technical pilot — monthly planning

| Component | Expected range |
|---|---:|
| Company email/collaboration | ₹2,000–₹5,000 |
| Telecom licences/concurrency | ₹5,000–₹10,000 |
| Number/call usage | ₹5,000–₹20,000 |
| Sarvam STT, 5,000 minutes | ₹2,500; ₹3,750 with diarisation |
| Cloud compute/database | ₹3,000–₹10,000 |
| Object storage/backups | ₹1,000–₹4,000 |
| Monitoring/email/security tools | ₹1,000–₹5,000 |
| Accounting and administration software | ₹0–₹3,000 |
| **Expected technical run cost** | **₹19,500–₹57,000/month** |

This excludes worker payments, coordinator costs, GST, legal advice and marketing.

Sarvam’s official current rate makes 5,000 minutes approximately ₹2,500 without diarisation. 

## C. Why not purchase all Zoho operational licences?

If we buy CRM, Desk, People, Shifts, Creator, Analytics and related licences for every flexible worker, licence costs grow even when workers are inactive.

The product’s economic advantage depends on:

- Pooled workers
- Variable activity
- Concurrent telephony
- Service-unit billing
- Minimal inactive-seat costs

Therefore, use SaaS only where it removes genuine regulatory or commodity burden.

---

# 10. Priority order

## P0 — Must happen before the product build

1. **Product charter**
2. **Service-company vs SaaS-company boundary**
3. **Work-item data model**
4. **Capability absorption matrix**
5. **Telecom proof with Exotel and Tata**
6. **Git repository and sprint-control structure**
7. **Security and tenancy baseline**
8. **Existing product code assessment**
9. **One-client pilot definition**
10. **Acceptance criteria**

## P0 — First build

2. Worker profile and skills
3. Availability and capacity
4. Work-item intake
5. Rules-based priority
6. Knowledge/customer timeline
7. Basic assignment
8. Worker execution console
9. Audit trail
10. Cost and margin calculation

## P1 — Next

1. Telecom integration
2. Call recording linkage
3. Sarvam batch transcription
4. Callback workflow
5. QA scorecards
6. Daily control tower
7. Worker payment statement
8. Client report
9. Basic alerts
10. Backup allocation

## P2 — After working pilot

1. Live transcription
2. Cross-client pre-emption
3. WhatsApp integration
4. AI-assisted classification
5. Client portal
6. Partner-company model

10. Language training/certification

## P3 — Scale stage

2. Advanced optimisation
3. Multi-country telephony
4. Enterprise WFM adapter
5. AI agent participation
6. White-label platform

---

# 11. Immediate tasks and deliverables

| No. | Deliverable | Purpose |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | Product Vision v1 | Freeze the problem and target users |
| 2 | Business Entity Boundary | Separate Ival operations, SaaS IP and partners |
| 3 | Service Catalogue v1 | Define the first four measurable services |
| 4 | Work-Item Canonical Model | One structure for call, ticket, data and software task |
| 5 | Priority & SLA Matrix | Explainable classification |
| 6 | Workforce Supply Model | Availability, skill, language and reliability |
| 7 | Client Demand Model | Volume, timing, skill and SLA |
| 8 | Functional Absorption Matrix | Buy/configure/integrate/build decision |
| 9 | Existing Code Reuse Audit | What can safely be copied or extracted |
| 10 | Telecom Vendor Proof | Indian DID, APIs, recording and media |
| 11 | STT Benchmark | Sarvam vs alternatives on real Tamil audio |
| 12 | Architecture Decision Record Set | Why each major technology was chosen |
| 13 | MVP Backlog | Prioritised user stories and acceptance tests |
| 14 | Cost Model | External software, infrastructure and delivery cost |
| 15 | Pilot Contract | Scope, SLA, data, price and limitations |
| 16 | Git/Sprint Structure | Durable project memory |
| 17 | Threat Model | Client isolation, remote worker and recording risks |
| 18 | End-to-End Prototype | One real work item from intake to billing |

---

# 12. The first proof—not the whole platform

The first proof should be:

> A customer calls an Indian number, chooses or states a reason, a callback work item is created, Tamil audio is transcribed, priority is assigned by rule, the best available certified worker receives it, calls back, records the outcome, and the system calculates client charge and worker earning.

This proves:

- Telecom
- Work intake
- Tamil STT
- Priority
- Availability
- Assignment
- Knowledge
- Execution
- Audit
- Billing

Everything else can grow from that vertical slice.

---

# 13. Final judgement

This brainstorming has produced a potentially valuable product idea, but it has **not yet proved that the market will purchase the product**.

The current outcome is not “we must build a huge platform.”

The correct outcome is:

> **We have enough discovery to freeze Version 1 of the problem, architecture and proof plan.**

The strongest immediate priorities are:

1. **Create the controlled product baseline.**
2. **Audit the existing product architecture and reusable components.**
3. **Prove Indian telecom integration.**
4. **Build the work-item + capacity + priority core.**
5. **Run Ival Puthiyaval as the first operational pilot.**
6. **Collect actual availability, handling time, quality, cost and client-demand data.**

Only after that should the broader distributed-work marketplace be treated as a committed product roadmap.

and client expects:

before client acquisition.

```text
Capacity Engine

```

```text
400

250

100
```

# The Question I Want You To Think About

This is probably the most important unresolved question from the whole discussion:

```text
What exactly is the unit of work?
```

Because everything depends on it.

For example:

BPO:

```text
Call
```

Recruitment:

```text
Candidate Screening
```

Software:

```text
User Story
```

Testing:

```text
Test Case
```

Marketing:

```text
Lead
```

Content:

```text
Video
```

If we cannot standardise the unit of work model, then:

```text
Capacity
Pricing
Allocation
Billing
Earnings
```

all become difficult.

I think the next discussion should focus on:

> **What is a work item? How do we define it universally across BPO, software, testing, marketing, content, recruitment and other services?**

Because that looks like the foundation stone for the entire platform.

# 17/07/2026 23:58 — Complete Product Baseline & Architecture

Partner, you are right: the earlier explanation and the generated diagram did not match properly. The diagram moved too quickly into a generic BPO platform and introduced items such as Asterisk, VICIdial and a complete communication layer without first preserving the exact reasoning developed in this discussion.

Below is the **full corrected explanation**, organised in one continuous structure.

---

# 1. What stage is this discussion?

This is **not random discussion**, but it is also **not yet formal architecture or development**.

The most accurate stage is:

> ## **Advanced Product Discovery and Concept Synthesis**

You have already passed the very early “idea” stage.

## Stage assessment

| Product stage | Current status | What it means |
|---|---:|---|
| Initial idea | Completed | The original idea was a women-empowerment BPO |
| Problem discovery | Advanced | Availability, pricing, handover, knowledge, telephony and quality problems are understood |
| Founder brainstorming | Advanced | Many business and technical scenarios have been explored |
| Business-model discovery | Advanced but not frozen | Service operator, SaaS company and partner network are emerging |
| Product discovery | Advanced | The core platform problem is visible |
| Market validation | Early | Comparable products were researched, but clients have not validated the full platform |
| Requirements baseline | Not yet frozen | Requirements exist across this long conversation |
| Architecture baseline | Ready to begin | Enough information exists to design Version 1 |
| MVP scope | Not frozen | Must follow the capability and buy/build decisions |
| Development | Not started for this exact product | Existing products may contain reusable code |
| Product-market fit | Not reached | Requires real workers, clients and usage data |

Therefore, the transition now is:

```text
Brainstorming
    ↓
Problem Discovery
    ↓
Business-Model Discovery
    ↓
Product Discovery
    ↓
CURRENT: Discovery Synthesis
    ↓
Requirements Baseline
    ↓
Architecture Baseline
    ↓
Proof of Concept
    ↓
MVP
```

Further brainstorming can continue, but every new point must now be captured in a controlled **Discovery Register**, not left only in chat history.

---

# 2. What are we actually building?

There are **three connected entities**.

## 2.1 Ival Puthiyaval Business Solutions

This is the social-enterprise service operator.

It will:

- Recruit and organise women
- Train workers
- Acquire clients
- Deliver BPO and digital services
- Monitor quality
- Handle client relationships
- Pay workers
- Demonstrate social impact

The current brochure presents Ival Puthiyaval as a conventional BPO offering customer support, telemarketing, data processing, finance support, HR support and content moderation. It also claims 24/7 worldwide support and a large trained workforce. The future model discussed here is substantially different and should eventually be reflected in the brochure only after it has been validated. fileciteturn2file0

---

## 2.2 Distributed Work Operating System

This is the SaaS product.

It will:

- Convert demand into structured work
- Divide large work into smaller work objects
- Record dependencies
- Match work to people or partner teams
- Prioritise urgent work
- Preserve knowledge
- Manage availability
- Calculate capacity
- Control QA and approval
- Calculate worker earnings
- Calculate client billing and profitability

---

## 2.3 Partner delivery network

This could later contain:

- Individual freelancers
- Women’s groups
- Five-person microbusinesses
- Specialist companies
- Testing teams
- Content teams
- Marketing teams
- Software-development teams
- Translation teams

They may:

- Work for Ival Puthiyaval
- Bring their own clients
- Use the SaaS platform
- Receive work through the network
- Operate independently while following common standards

---

# 3. The foundation: Universal Work Object

You correctly said that work can be divided similarly to Agile:

```text
Portfolio
  ↓
Programme
  ↓
Project / Product
  ↓
Epic
  ↓
Story
  ↓
Task
  ↓
Subtask
```

However, Jira’s exact hierarchy cannot simply be copied because this product must support BPO, recruitment, data processing, testing, content, marketing and other work.

The platform therefore needs a universal object:

> ## **Work Object**

A Work Object can be:

- A complete client engagement
- An ongoing service
- A campaign
- A call
- A customer complaint
- A transcript-validation activity
- A QA review
- A manager approval
- A software user story
- A test case
- A video script
- A recruitment screening
- A document-verification task

## 3.1 Work Objects can contain other Work Objects

Example:

```text
Customer Support Campaign
  ├── Customer Call
  │    ├── Live Conversation
  │    ├── Transcript
  │    ├── Disposition
  │    ├── Follow-up
  │    └── QA Review
  ├── Complaint Investigation
  └── Weekly Performance Review
```

Example for software:

```text
Product Epic
  ├── User Story
  │    ├── Design
  │    ├── Backend Development
  │    ├── Frontend Development
  │    ├── Unit Test
  │    ├── Code Review
  │    ├── Playwright Test
  │    └── Deployment Verification
```

## 3.2 Every Work Object should contain

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Work Object ID | Permanent unique identity |
| Parent ID | Supports hierarchy |
| Type | Call, review, development, screening, etc. |
| Organisation/client | Who owns the work |
| Customer/subject | Who or what the work concerns |
| Objective | Required business result |
| Input | Data, call, file, lead or instruction |
| Required skills | Who is qualified |
| Required language | Language capability |
| Priority | Urgency |
| SLA/due time | Deadline |
| Estimated effort | Capacity planning |
| Actual effort | Cost and productivity |
| Dependencies | What must happen first |
| Worker/team | Current assignee |
| Knowledge reference | SOP, customer history and rules |
| Evidence | How completion is proved |
| QA requirement | Review level |
| Approval rule | Who closes it |
| Client charge | Revenue |
| Worker earning | Delivery payment |
| Status | Current lifecycle stage |
| Audit history | Every change and decision |

This Work Object is the core foundation of the product.

---
