# Correct sprint execution loop

Each sprint must run as one controlled loop:

```text
Read approved sprint documents
        ↓
Confirm sprint scope
        ↓
Create branch
        ↓
Implement code
        ↓
Create or update tests
        ↓
Run static checks
        ↓
Run unit tests
        ↓
Run integration tests
        ↓
Run API tests
        ↓
Run functional tests
        ↓
Run negative tests
        ↓
Run Playwright tests
        ↓
Check coverage
        ↓
Fix failures
        ↓
Repeat all affected tests
        ↓
Update documentation and evidence
        ↓
Git commit
        ↓
Git push
        ↓
Run CI
        ↓
If CI fails, fix and push again
        ↓
If all gates pass, close sprint
        ↓
Start next sprint
```

## Important correction

Do not commit only once at the very end.

Use meaningful checkpoint commits during development, but only mark the sprint complete after all quality gates pass.

Example:

```text
feat(auth): add login domain and API contract
```

```text
test(auth): add unit and negative authentication cases
```

```text
test(e2e): add Playwright login and lockout scenarios
```

```text
fix(auth): correct token-expiry handling
```

```text
docs(sprint-01): update RTM, evidence and sprint status
```

Then push and allow CI to verify the integrated branch.

---

# What Git should track

You are correct that Git itself should show the real effort and development history.

Every sprint should make the repository history reveal:

- what was implemented
- when it was implemented
- what tests were added
- what failed
- what was fixed
- which requirement was addressed
- which documents changed
- which sprint was completed
- which release contains the work

## Required Git artefacts

```text
Source code
Tests
Playwright scripts
Database migrations
API schemas
Configuration
CI workflows
Documentation updates
RTM updates
Sprint status
ADRs
Test evidence summaries
Release notes
```

Large generated evidence such as videos, full test traces, screenshots and binary reports should normally go into CI artefacts or release artefacts rather than bloating Git history. Git should retain the scripts, configuration, summaries and links or identifiers needed to reproduce the evidence.

---

# Coverage rule

Be careful with the phrase **100% coverage**.

Codex can be required to target and report 100% for agreed coverage scopes, but raw line coverage alone does not prove quality.

The sprint gate should require:

```text
100% requirements traceability
100% acceptance-criteria coverage
100% critical-path functional coverage
100% identified negative-case execution
100% planned Playwright scenario execution
No open Critical or High defects
Configured code-coverage threshold passed
```

For code coverage:

- Domain and business-rule code can reasonably target 100%.
- Overall repository coverage may require exclusions for generated code, framework glue, platform bootstrap and defensive branches that cannot be meaningfully executed.
- Every exclusion must be documented and approved.
- Mutation testing should be used for critical business logic where practical, because it proves whether tests actually detect broken behaviour.

So Codex must never achieve “100%” by writing weak tests or excluding difficult code.

---

# Development-page opening prompt

Use this as the first message in Page 2 for each project.

## UraiFlow development page prompt

**Page name**

```text
02-Sprint-Development-Test-and-Release
```

**Prompt**

```text
You are the permanent engineering lead and Codex execution controller for UraiFlow.

The approved planning ZIP uploaded to this project is the implementation source of truth.

This page owns the complete sprint lifecycle:

- coding
- test creation
- test execution
- defect correction
- Git commits
- Git pushes
- CI verification
- documentation updates
- sprint closure
- release preparation

Never separate development from testing.

Every sprint must include implementation and all applicable tests before it can be closed.

For each sprint:

1. Read the sprint tracker, sprint context pack, module planner, RTM, architecture, API contracts, data model, ADRs and testing requirements.

2. State:
   - sprint number
   - sprint objective
   - in-scope requirements
   - out-of-scope items
   - affected modules
   - affected documents
   - required tests
   - migration impact
   - risks
   - assumptions or open decisions

3. Do not invent missing requirements.
   If a required decision is absent or documents conflict, stop and create an Open Decision report.

4. Create or use the correct Git branch for the sprint.

5. Implement only the approved sprint scope.

6. Create and maintain all applicable:
   - unit tests
   - component tests
   - integration tests
   - API contract tests
   - database tests
   - functional tests
   - negative tests
   - permission tests
   - security tests
   - Playwright end-to-end tests
   - regression tests

7. Execute:
   - formatting
   - linting
   - static analysis
   - type checking
   - dependency checks
   - secret scanning
   - unit tests
   - integration tests
   - functional tests
   - negative tests
   - Playwright tests
   - coverage checks
   - build validation

8. If any check fails:
   - record the failure
   - identify the root cause
   - fix the code or test
   - rerun all affected checks
   - do not close the sprint

9. Maintain meaningful Git history.
   Use small, traceable commits with requirement and sprint references where practical.

10. Push the branch and verify CI.

11. If CI fails:
   - diagnose
   - correct
   - commit
   - push again
   - rerun CI

12. A sprint is complete only when:
   - all acceptance criteria pass
   - all planned tests pass
   - CI passes
   - coverage gates pass
   - no Critical or High defects remain
   - RTM is updated
   - sprint tracker is updated
   - ADRs are updated where required
   - help and technical documentation are updated
   - release notes are updated
   - implementation evidence is recorded

13. Never mark work complete based only on code generation.

14. Never proceed to the next sprint until the current sprint passes all gates or I explicitly approve an exception.

15. At the end of every sprint, create:

Codex-YYYYMMDDHHMM-UraiFlow-Sprint-XX-Implementation-and-Test-Report-v01.md

The report must contain:
- planned scope
- delivered scope
- files changed
- requirements covered
- tests created
- test results
- coverage results
- failures and fixes
- Git commits
- branch and push status
- CI result
- known limitations
- deferred items
- documentation updated
- final sprint verdict

Wait for the approved ZIP and repository before starting implementation.
```

---

## Parent platform development page prompt

Use the same prompt, replacing:

```text
UraiFlow
```

with:

```text
AUMNITY-Work-Platform
```

And use the output filename:

```text
Codex-YYYYMMDDHHMM-AUMNITY-Work-Platform-Sprint-XX-Implementation-and-Test-Report-v01.md
```

# Final structure

```text
AT-UraiFlow
├── 01-Architecture-Review-and-Planning
└── 02-Sprint-Development-Test-and-Release
```

```text
AT-AUMNITY-Work-Platform
├── 01-Architecture-Review-and-Planning
└── 02-Sprint-Development-Test-and-Release
```

That is the clean structure. The second page is not merely “development.” It is the complete **code → test → fix → commit → push → CI → verify → next sprint** execution page.

## Advantages

This gives you one place to change the process.

Example:

Today:

```text
Every sprint requires Playwright tests.
```

Six months later:

```text
Every sprint requires

Playwright

+
Mutation Testing

+
Performance Benchmark
```

Only one document changes.

All future sprints automatically follow the new rule.

---

Instead of the AI remembering from previous chats:

```

```

it reads:

```
Engineering Procedure v1.4

Step 17

Run Playwright tests.

Mandatory.
```

Much more reliable.


For example:

1. ADRs
2. Approved Architecture
3. Engineering Procedures
4. Sprint Context Packs
5. Sprint Tracker
6. Module Planner
7. AI Prompt

So if an AI prompt contradicts the engineering procedure, the documented procedure wins.
